					<description><![CDATA[<p>Intel's first 18A-P external customer; Siemens buys Breker; backside clock distribution; Microsoft taps EMIB; export controls tighten around China; Nvidia expands packaging capacity; SC26 brings new HPC HW; JEDEC previews DDR6; Europe pushes ahead with €33B in chip projects.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/chip-industry-week-in-review-165/">Chip Industry Week In Review</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<h3><strong>Deals and buys</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Intel Foundry</strong> signed its <a href="https://x.com/magicsilicon/status/2123095994770272562">first external customer for its advanced 18A-P</a> semiconductor manufacturing process, according to Intel executives. The customer has not been identified, and Intel has not disclosed a production timetable.</li>
<li><strong>Siemens</strong> plans to <a href="https://news.siemens.com/en-us/siemens-to-acquire-breker-verification-systems/">acquire</a> <strong>Breker Verification Systems</strong>, a developer of AI-driven test suite synthesis and system-level verification technologies. The acquisition is expected to close in December.</li>
<li><strong>Microsoft</strong> selected <strong>Intel Foundry</strong>&#8216;s <a href="https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/newsroom/news/corporate/microsoft-azure-infrastructure-collaboration.html">EMIB advanced packaging technology</a> for custom silicon used in Azure&#8217;s infrastructure. The multiyear agreement also expands cooperation on Xeon processors, memory, and security technologies.</li>
<li><strong>Infineon</strong> and <strong>Megmeet</strong> are <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202611-034">teaming up on 800V DC power architectures</a> for AI data centers, using Infineon&#8217;s gen-2 CoolSiC MOSFETs in three-phase power shelves.</li>
<li><strong>GlobalFoundries </strong>and <strong>Boston Dynamics</strong> <a href="https://gf.com/news-and-events/news/boston-dynamics-and-globalfoundries-expand-robotics-collaboration/">expanded their robotics collaboration</a> to deploy autonomous Spot inspection robots across fabs in the U.S., Germany, and Singapore. GF also plans to begin evaluating Boston Dynamics&#8217; Atlas humanoid robots for semiconductor manufacturing tasks in early 2027.</li>
<li><strong>AMD</strong> completed acquisitions of AI inference chip developer <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/amd-completes-acquisition-taalas-advance-ai-inference-innovation/">Taalas</a> and AI model research company <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/amd-completes-acquisition-world-labs/">World Labs</a>.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Funding</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li>The <strong>European Commission</strong> said more than 20 semiconductor projects have been approved under the EU Chips Act, representing over <a href="https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/news/commission-marks-more-20-investments-boost-semiconductor-supply">€33B in public and private investment</a>.</li>
<li><strong>General Flash</strong> raised $128M to scale production of <a href="https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/general-flash-announces-128-million-in-funding-to-scale-us-production-of-critical-metals-302941551.html">critical metals</a> used in semiconductor and other industries.</li>
<li><strong>Atom Computing</strong> <a href="https://atom-computing.com/atom-computing-finalizes-100-million-u-s-department-of-commerce-rd-award-to-accelerate-fault-tolerant-utility-scale-quantum-computing/">finalized</a> an up to $100M CHIPS Act R&amp;D award for neutral-atom quantum computers.</li>
<li><strong>Science Corporation</strong> raised $400M for its <a href="https://science.xyz/news/series-d/">retinal implant chip</a> that aims to restore form vision for certain types of macular degeneration.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Chip trade and export controls</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li>The <strong>U.S. Department of Commerce</strong> <a href="https://www.bis.gov/press-release/bis-issues-rule-addressing-remote-access-american-super-intelligence-technology">introduced new restrictions</a> addressing remote access to advanced U.S. AI computing through overseas data centers, along with new semiconductor licensing requirements affecting Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, Indonesia, and Brazil.</li>
<li>The U.S. received license requests covering 3.4 million advanced <strong>Nvidia</strong> and <strong>AMD</strong> AI chips for <a href="https://foreignaffairs.house.gov/news/press-releases/chairman-brian-mast-exercises-legislative-authority-to-publicly-release-ai-chip-license-information-for-china">China and approved more than 879,000</a>, according to U.S. government data. It is unclear how many were actually shipped.</li>
<li><strong>TSMC</strong> and <strong>Samsung</strong> reportedly <a href="https://www.ft.com/content/d42dd235-7b0e-4651-a8d2-f7b7b4963a74">suspended some chip shipments to Chinese customers</a> following new U.S. export restrictions, pending licensing approvals, per the Financial Times. TSMC is also reportedly <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/china/tsmc-tightens-china-ai-chip-curbs-new-us-rules-close-manufacturing-loophole-2026-11-19/">halting affected Chinese customer orders at 16nm and below</a> without U.S. approval and generally limiting those customers to 28nm and 40nm processes, provided those chips do not use HBM or advanced packaging.</li>
<li>The U.S. government <a href="https://www.justice.gov/opa/pr/joint-statement-department-commerce-and-department-justice-megaspeed-investigation">released new details</a> of an ongoing investigation into <strong>Megaspeed</strong> over suspected semiconductor export-control violations. <strong>Nvidia</strong> placed Megaspeed on its no-sell list in June 2025, but its Malaysian subsidiary, <strong>Speedmatrix</strong>, later received more than <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/17/business/economy/megaspeed-investigation-trump-doj.html">$800M in Nvidia AI technology</a>, mostly through third-party manufacturers, per the NYT. Nvidia says it blocked new orders and obtained government authorization for certain repair parts shipments. An investigation is underway.</li>
<li><strong>The Trump administration</strong> is moving toward restrictions on <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/trumps-china-data-center-curb-advances-despite-amazon-google-meta-lobbying-2026-11-17/">Chinese optical transceiver imports</a>, despite opposition from major U.S. cloud companies, reported Reuters.</li>
</ul>
<h2 role="heading" aria-level="2" data-uw-rm-heading="level"><a href="http://hms.ceratizit.com/jatnEn"><img data-recalc-dims="1" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24281643" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/onlinebanner_HMS-Semiconductor_1270x240px_V1.jpg?resize=968%2C184&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="968" height="184" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/onlinebanner_HMS-Semiconductor_1270x240px_V1.jpg?resize=300%2C57&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/onlinebanner_HMS-Semiconductor_1270x240px_V1.jpg?resize=1024%2C194&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/onlinebanner_HMS-Semiconductor_1270x240px_V1.jpg?resize=768%2C145&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/onlinebanner_HMS-Semiconductor_1270x240px_V1.jpg?resize=600%2C113&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/onlinebanner_HMS-Semiconductor_1270x240px_V1.jpg?w=1270&amp;ssl=1 1270w" sizes="(max-width: 968px) 100vw, 968px" /></a></h2>
<h3><strong>Manufacturing capacity</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Nvidia</strong> <a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-expands-commitments-with-supply-chain-partners-to-support-ai-infrastructure-buildout">expanded its manufacturing agreements</a> with <strong>Amkor</strong>, <strong>Samsung Electro-Mechanics</strong>, <strong>SPIL</strong>, <strong>TSMC</strong>, and others. The commitments cover additional substrate manufacturing, advanced packaging, and wafer capacity. As part of the expansion, <strong>ASE </strong>subsidiary <strong>SPIL</strong> signed a <a href="https://www.spilglobal.com/NewsDetail/63c1aba5d14bac8c">multibillion-dollar agreement</a> with <strong>Nvidia</strong> to triple its advanced semiconductor packaging capacity in Taiwan by mid-2029, including new production lines for wafer bumping, assembly, and testing.</li>
<li><strong>ASE Technology</strong> inaugurated the first phase and began construction of the second phase of its <a href="https://www.aseglobal.com/press-room-ch/lujhu-plant-phase-1-inauguration-and-phase-2-groundbreaking">Lujhu advanced packaging facility</a> in Kaohsiung, Taiwan.</li>
<li><strong>Lam Research</strong> broke ground on a <a href="https://www.cna.com.tw/business/chinese/450453">technology center</a> in <strong>Zhunan</strong>,<strong> Taiwan</strong>, as part of its five-year, $3B global R&amp;D expansion.</li>
<li><strong>GlobalFoundries</strong> broke ground on a <a href="https://investors.gf.com/news-releases/news-release-details/globalfoundries-breaks-ground-first-new-semiconductor-fab-under">new 300mm semiconductor fab</a> in <strong>Malta, New York</strong>, under a U.S.-Japan investment initiative providing up to $21B for two fabs. The first 300mm fab is expected to begin production in 2029.</li>
<li><strong>ADEKA</strong> and <strong>Entegris</strong> <a href="https://www.adeka.co.jp/en/news/2026/11/261116-e.html">agreed to collaborate</a> on advanced semiconductor materials production in Japan.</li>
<li><strong>Silicon Box</strong> advanced its proposed <a href="https://www.silicon-box.com/silicon-box-semiconductor-manufacturing-facility-in-italy-enters-basic-design-phase">semiconductor packaging and test facility</a> in <strong>Novara, Italy</strong>, into the basic design stage. The project is intended to establish additional European advanced packaging capacity for chiplet-based semiconductor devices.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>SC26</strong></h3>
<p>The annual supercomputing conference kicked off this week in Chicago.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Nvidia</strong> announced a Rubin <a href="https://blogs.nvidia.com/blog/rubin-hpc/">HPC GPU</a> with more than 50,000 CUDA cores on a single GPU, along with sparse data acceleration capabilities. It also announced a 192-CPU Vera <a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-expands-vera-cpu-platform-to-accelerate-scientific-discovery">rack-scale platform</a> for HPC with more than 200TB/s of aggregate peak memory bandwidth.</li>
<li><strong>Penguin Solutions</strong> unveiled a <a href="https://ir.penguinsolutions.com/news/news-details/2026/Penguin-Solutions-Unveils-Industrys-First-Photonics-Based-HBM-Class-Memory-Appliance-to-Break-the-GPU-Memory-Wall-for-Agentic-AI/default.aspx">photonics-based HBM-class memory appliance</a> that uses <strong>Marvell</strong> technology to enable CPUs and GPUs to share memory across servers.</li>
<li><strong>Lambda</strong> is <a href="https://blogs.nvidia.com/blog/lambda-photonics/">deploying Nvidia&#8217;s CPO switches</a> to connect GB300 NVL72 racks with more than 10,000 GPUs. Nvidia claims up to 5× better power efficiency and 15× higher reliability than conventional pluggable optics.</li>
<li><strong>AMD</strong> announced that its <a href="https://newsroom.amd.com/news/amd-powered-lux-supercomputer-opens-scientific-users/">Lux supercomputer</a> at <strong>ORNL</strong>, powered by EPYC CPUs and Instinct MI355X GPUs, is now open to scientific users.</li>
</ul>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24284391 size-full" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.18.44-AM.png?resize=741%2C580&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="741" height="580" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.18.44-AM.png?w=741&amp;ssl=1 741w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.18.44-AM.png?resize=300%2C235&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.18.44-AM.png?resize=600%2C470&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 741px) 100vw, 741px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 1: Chicago’s SC26 event this week. Source: Liz Allan/Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<h3><strong>Reports and analysis</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>NAND flash contract prices</strong> are <a href="https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20261119-13314.html">expected to decline in 2027</a> as Chinese cloud providers cut enterprise SSD orders amid GPU shortages and high inventories, reports <strong>TrendForce</strong>. Global NAND capital spending is projected to rise ~27% to ~US$30B.</li>
<li><strong>CSIS</strong> examined the <a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/between-rock-and-hard-place-evaluating-risks-chinese-memory-chips">risks of using Chinese memory chips</a>, including <strong>CXMT</strong> DRAM, citing security and supply-chain concerns. The report recommends restricting their use in sensitive government systems and expanding independent chip testing.</li>
<li>China still <a href="https://www.uscc.gov/sites/default/files/2026-11/Chapter_8--Silicon_to_Sovereignty_Chinas_Bid_to_Control_the_AI_Tech_Stack.pdf">trails in leading-edge semiconductor fabrication, HBM, and chip manufacturing equipment</a>, but it&#8217;s trying to work around those gaps through DUV multi-patterning, advanced packaging, 3D stacking, and photonics, said the <strong>US-China Economic and Security Review Commission</strong> in its annual report to Congress.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Standards</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>JEDEC</strong> previewed the <a href="https://www.jedec.org/news/pressreleases/jedec%C2%AE-previews-next-generation-ddr6-dram-higher-performance-greater-capacity">DDR6 specification</a>. While still subject to change, the current plan includes higher data rates and a wider bus, a pseudo-channel architecture, optimizations for die stacking and high-capacity modules, and data-center-focused low-power modes.</li>
<li>JEDEC also started a <a href="https://www.jedec.org/news/pressreleases/jedec%C2%AE-announces-creation-jc-148-subcommittee-system-level-reliability-testing">System Level Reliability Testing</a> subcommittee to develop qualification methodologies, reliability practices, and evaluation approaches for emerging system applications.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Earnings and chip stocks</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Year-over-year revenue change</strong>: <strong>Keysight</strong> Q4 <a href="https://s22.q4cdn.com/444849635/files/doc_earnings/2026/q4/earnings-result/Q4-26-Earnings-Results.pdf">up 40%</a>; <strong>Kulicke &amp; Soffa</strong> Q4 <a href="https://investor.kns.com/2026-11-18-Kulicke-Soffa-Reports-Fourth-Quarter-2026-Results">up 131%;</a> <strong>Nvidia</strong> Q3 <a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-announces-financial-results-for-third-quarter-fiscal-2027">up 103%;</a> and <strong>Soitec</strong> Q2 <a href="https://www.soitec.com/home/group/corporate/newsroom/press-releases/content/2026/11/18/soitec-reports-second-quarter-revenue-and-half-year-results-of-fiscal-year-2027">up 50%</a> (at constant currency).<!-- BEGIN QUICK LINKS --></li>
<li><strong>Notable</strong>: Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress said during the earnings call that the company has <a href="https://www.fool.com/earnings/call-transcripts/2026/11/18/nvidia-nvda-q3-2027-earnings-call-transcript/">stopped shipping licensed Hopper AI chips to China,</a> and its Q4 outlook assumes <a href="https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Announces-Financial-Results-for-Third-Quarter-Fiscal-2027/">no data center compute</a> revenue from China. The company also established an <a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-board-of-directors-establishes-national-security-and-export-control-oversight-committee">oversight committee</a> for national security policies, export control compliance, and global trade risk management.</li>
<li>Check out the <a href="https://semiengineering.com/business-news-startups/#stocks">comprehensive chip stock chart</a> for the latest impacts.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>People</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Cirrus Logic</strong> President/CEO <strong>John Forsyth</strong> was <a href="https://www.semiconductors.org/john-forsyth-cirrus-logic-president-and-ceo-elected-chair-of-semiconductor-industry-association/">elected chair</a> of the <strong>Semiconductor Industry Association</strong>, succeeding <strong>AMD </strong>CEO <strong>Lisa Su</strong>. <strong>GlobalFoundries</strong> Executive Chairman <strong>Tom Caulfield</strong> was elected vice chair.</li>
</ul>

<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;"><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#Global"><strong>Global</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— U.S. equipment expansion in Oregon; photonics R&amp;D in Finland and Japan; SK hynix adds test capacity.</span></div>
<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;"><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#New%20Technologies"><strong>New Technologies</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— AI-assisted chip design; high-power test; panel-level bonding; cloud-based engineering simulation; secure auto Ethernet.</span></div>
<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;"><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#Security"><strong>Security</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— AMD PSP flaw; DDR5 rowhammer; GPU side channels; memory DoS attacks.</span></div>
<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;"><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#Trending%20Video"><strong>Trending Video</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— Physics vs. data in simulation; process variability; memory and packaging optimization.</span></div>
<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;"><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#Research"><strong>Research</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— Backside clock delivery; 2D/1D CMOS; AI-chip liquid cooling; ferroelectric memory. </span></div>
<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;"><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#Quantum%20Hardware"><strong>Quantum Hardware</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— FPGA quantum control; real-time error correction; quantum safety; quantum-classical HPC.</span></div>
<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;"><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#Workforce,%20Education"><strong>Workforce | Education</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— Student HPC competition in Chicago; New York training deal; new talent hub in Arizona.</span></div>
<div style="width: 48.5%; box-sizing: border-box; font-size: 16px; font-family: inherit; line-height: 1.4;">
<p><a style="font-size: inherit; font-weight: bold; color: #174b91;" href="#Events%20and%20Webinars"><strong>Events and Webinars</strong></a> <span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;"><span style="font-size: 15px; font-weight: 400; color: #444;">— Boston materials conference; </span></span><span style="font-family: inherit;">AI data-center cooling; UCIe chiplets; GAA NSFET design.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<h2 id="Global"><strong>Global</strong></h2>
<h3><strong>Americas</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>ACM Research</strong> opened a <a href="https://ir.acmr.com/news-releases/news-release-details/acm-research-expands-us-operations-oregon-rd-and-manufacturing">new research and manufacturing facility</a> in <strong>Hillsboro</strong>,<strong> Oregon</strong>, as part of a U.S. expansion. The facility supports development and production activities associated with the company&#8217;s semiconductor wet-cleaning and electroplating equipment.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Europe</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>imec</strong> opened <a href="https://www.imec-int.com/en/press/imec-finland-espoo-rd-center-opens-accelerate-integrated-photonics-and-optical-system">a photonics R&amp;D center</a> in <strong>Espoo</strong>, <strong>Finland</strong>, expanding its capabilities in optical design, simulation, testing, and system development.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Asia</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>imec</strong> established an <a href="https://www.imec-int.com/en/press/imec-sets-rd-activities-hokkaido-strengthen-japans-next-generation-semiconductor-and">R&amp;D presence</a> in <strong>Hokkaido, Japan</strong>, focused on photonic ICs, electronic-photonic integration, and CPO for AI and HPC.</li>
<li><strong>SK hynix</strong> detailed plans for a jointly funded ₩874.6B (US$655M) <a href="https://news.bizwatch.co.kr/article/industry/2026/11/16/0001">semiconductor testing facility</a> at its Yongin complex in <strong>South Korea</strong>. The cleanroom is scheduled to open in May 2027.</li>
</ul>
<div style="background: #f7f7f7; border: 1.5px solid #888888; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 24px 0; border-radius: 3px;">
<h2 id="In-Depth"><strong>In-Depth</strong></h2>
<p><strong>Semiconductor Engineering</strong> published the <a href="https://semiengineering.com/newsletter/manufacturing-packaging-materials-nov-2026/">Manufacturing, Packaging and Materials</a> newsletter this week, including:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Special Report:</strong> <a href="https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-make-its-own-manufacturing-decisions/">Can AI Make Its Own Manufacturing Decisions</a>?</li>
<li><a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-accelerators-drive-changes-in-power-delivery-networks/">AI Accelerators Drive Changes In Power Delivery Networks</a></li>
<li><a href="https://semiengineering.com/detachable-fiber-connectors-pose-new-challenges-for-photonics/">Detachable Fiber Connectors Pose New Challenges For Photonics</a></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Plus</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Special Report</strong>: <a href="https://semiengineering.com/are-ai-agents-the-new-semiconductor-ip/">Are AI Agents The New Semiconductor IP</a>?</li>
<li><strong>Tech Talk</strong>: <a href="https://semiengineering.com/physics-vs-data/">Physics Vs. Data</a></li>
<li><strong>Roundtable: </strong><a href="https://semiengineering.com/can-ai-agents-really-shift-chip-design-left/">Can AI Agents Really Shift Chip Design Left</a>?</li>
</ul>
</div>
<hr />
<h2 id="New Technologies"><strong>New Technologies</strong></h2>
<p><strong>OpenAI</strong> reported advances in <a href="https://openai.com/index/advancing-chip-design-with-our-models/">AI-assisted chip design</a>, including RTL generation and design optimization for <strong>AMD</strong> processors and RISC-V cores. It is also working with <strong>Synopsys</strong> to train AI models to use EDA tools.</p>
<p><strong>Advantest</strong> <a href="https://www.advantest.com/en/news/2026/20261117.html">expanded its V93000 EXA Scale test platform</a> with the new EXA Scale QX test head for high-power, high-pin-count AI and HPC devices. The four-card-cage architecture provides 36 instrument slots, increasing test capacity while maintaining compatibility with existing V93000 instruments, wafer probers, and test handlers. The system is expected to become available in Q1 FY2027.</p>
<p><strong>Kulicke &amp; Soffa</strong> shipped its first <a href="https://investor.kns.com/2026-11-18-Kulicke-Soffa-Accelerates-Industry-Shift-to-Panel-Level-Capability-for-AI-Applications">fluxless thermocompression bonding systems for panel-level packaging</a> to two customers in Taiwan, supporting larger, higher-density packages for AI and HPC chips.</p>
<p><strong>Siemens</strong> and <strong>Microsoft</strong> are planning to bring <a href="https://news.siemens.com/en-us/simcenter-x-on-azure/">Simcenter X to Microsoft Azure</a>, enabling engineering teams in automotive and other industries to run multi-domain simulations and scale HPC workloads in the cloud.</p>
<p><strong>Keysight</strong> expanded its <a href="https://www.keysight.com/us/en/about/newsroom/catalog/news-release.2026.1117_pr26-124-keysight-brings-multi-unmanned-aerial-vehicle-6g-sensing-into-the-lab-with-cohere-technologies.html">6G sensing and communications testing capabilities</a> with <strong>Cohere Technologies</strong>&#8216; Zak-OTFS technology to simulate multiple drones and their wireless communications under realistic radio conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Infineon</strong> introduced a <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202611-031">TMR-based magnetic switch</a>, combining ultra-low current consumption with direct load switching in a single component. Applications include glucose monitoring, smart glasses, AR/VR devices, and medical disposables.</p>
<p><strong>Microsoft</strong> made its <strong>Arm</strong> Neoverse CSS V3-based Azure Cobalt 200 <a href="https://newsroom.arm.com/news/arm-microsoft-azure-cobalt-200">virtual machines generally available for cloud and AI workloads</a>.</p>
<h3><strong>Vehicles</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Rambus</strong> introduced <a href="https://www.rambus.com/blogs/extending-ethernet-security-to-the-automotive-edge/">security IP for 10 Mbps automotive Ethernet</a> connections to edge nodes, built for 10BASE-T1S/T1L with low, predictable latency and up to 3× area savings.</li>
<li><strong>Infineon</strong>&#8216;s <a href="https://www.infineon.com/technology-news/2026/inftn202611-033">silicon carbide power module for drivetrain inverters</a> is now available, extending its HybridPACK HD portfolio to heavy-duty applications such as long-haul transport, electric buses, and coaches.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2 id="Research"><strong>Research</strong></h2>
<p>A<strong> KAIST</strong> team created a <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20414">clock distribution</a> network with a backside clock mesh in BSPDN architecture, with experiments demonstrating &#8220;26% lower maximum local clock latency, 40% smaller global skew, and 29% less mesh switching power on average than the frontside baseline.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Nanjing University</strong> researchers developed <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41928-026-01737-7">CMOS circuits with 2D MoS₂ and 1D carbon nanotube</a> transistors.</p>
<p>Researchers at <strong>UC Irvine</strong> and <strong>Samsung Electronics</strong> created <a href="https://doi.org/10.1109/TCPMT.2026.3751185">topology-optimized liquid-cooling cold plates</a> to reduce thermal crosstalk between AI processors and HBM.</p>
<p>Researchers at <strong>Tampere University</strong> and the <strong>University of Southampton</strong> developed <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12968">wafer-scale ferroelectric transistors using MoS₂ and hafnium-zirconium oxide (HZO)</a> for nonvolatile, multibit memory.</p>
<p><strong>Uppsala University</strong> researchers proposed using co-packaged optics to <a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2611.17097v1">broadcast LLM weights from centralized memory</a>, reducing HBM requirements.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Security"><strong>Security</strong></h2>
<p><strong>SEALSQ</strong> signed a non-binding letter of intent to acquire Australia-based QuintessenceLabs, combining secure chips with quantum <a href="https://www.quintessencelabs.com/news/sealsq-signs-a-loi-to-acquire-quintessencelabs">random number generation and enterprise key management</a>.</p>
<h3><strong>Alerts and announcements</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>AMD</strong> disclosed a critical <a href="https://www.amd.com/en/resources/product-security/bulletin/amd-sb-7075.html?">Platform Security Processor boot ROM vulnerability</a> affecting Zen/Zen+ processors that can let attacker-controlled firmware bypass secure-boot authentication and execute unsigned code with full PSP privileges. AMD does not plan to release a mitigation because the vulnerable boot ROM cannot be modified.</li>
<li><strong>Intel</strong> issued a <a href="https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/security-center/announcement/intel-security-announcement-2026-11-16-002.html">security announcement on a DDR5 rowhammer attack</a> that researchers used to bypass certain protections, induce bit flips on systems with 12th- and 13th-gen Core processors, and escalate privileges. Intel recommends keeping platform firmware, BIOS, and operating systems current.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Security Research</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li>A<strong> University of Luxembourg</strong> team identified new <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23022">security risks in Nvidia GPU load/store units</a>. Two timing side channels enabled full AES-128 key recovery and covert communication between processes under Nvidia&#8217;s Multi-Process Service (MPS).</li>
<li><strong>University of Kansas</strong> and <strong>Cornell</strong> researchers demonstrated a practical <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16189">low-bandwidth memory denial-of-service attack</a> that caused more than 1,000× slowdowns on DDR5- and HBM2E-based systems while consuming less than 1 GB/s of memory bandwidth.</li>
<li><strong>TU Berlin</strong> researchers demonstrated a <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18126">physical attack on firmware TPM protections</a> that can reset failed-login counters and bypass PIN lockout protections used by Windows Hello and BitLocker.</li>
</ul>
<h3><strong>Government security news</strong></h3>
<ul>
<li>Global government cybersecurity agencies in several countries warned about a <a href="https://content.govdelivery.com/accounts/USDHSCISA/bulletins/4332a87">Java/OpenJDK flaw</a> that can allow remote code execution on affected x86-64 systems. CISA urged immediate patching.</li>
<li>The <strong>UK</strong> opened applications for a £2.4M open-source semiconductor security program for <a href="https://www.ukri.org/opportunity/open-source-semiconductor-security-platform-development/">secure-by-design hardware technologies</a>.</li>
<li><strong>CISA</strong> alerts are <a href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories">here</a>.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2 id="Trending Video"><strong>Trending Video</strong></h2>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/physics-vs-data/">Physics Vs. Data</a>: <strong>Lam Research</strong>&#8216;s David Fried talks about different approaches to speed up simulations, how to account for process variability with angstrom-level accuracy, and how to optimize systems for different memories and packaging options.</p>
<p><iframe src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/v5me-YHuHhc" width="480" height="270" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen="allowfullscreen"></iframe></p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Quantum Hardware"><strong>Quantum Hardware</strong></h2>
<p><strong>Altera</strong>, <strong>OQC</strong>, and <strong>Nvidia</strong> demonstrated an <a href="https://www.altera.com/newsroom/quantum-system-controller-reference-design">FPGA-based quantum controller</a> that uses an Agilex 9 Direct RF FPGA to connect a superconducting QPU to Nvidia GPUs for real-time control and readout. The reference design reduces the need for external RF conversion hardware.</p>
<p><strong>Quantum Machines</strong> demonstrated <a href="https://www.quantum-machines.co/press-release/real-time-qec-decoding-opx1000-nvidia-cuda-q/">real-time quantum error-correction processing</a> using its OPX1000 quantum controller and Nvidia CUDA-Q over NVQLink. Tests using simulated error data achieved 2.4 microseconds of round-trip latency with a CPU-based decoder. GPU-based decoding is planned.</p>
<p><strong>IBM</strong>, <strong>Nvidia</strong>, and <strong>Fermi National Accelerator Laboratory</strong> announced plans for a <a href="https://newsroom.ibm.com/2026-11-16-ibm,-fermilab,-and-nvidia-announce-plans-for-new-co-design-platform-to-accelerate-the-development-of-next-generation,-modular-quantum-computers">quantum-classical co-design platform</a>, combining IBM quantum computers with Nvidia DGX B200 systems to research modular quantum architectures, scientific applications, and supercomputing integration.</p>
<hr />
<h2 id="Workforce, Education"><strong>Workforce, Education</strong></h2>
<p><span data-subtree="aimfl" data-processed="true">A live 48-hour </span><a href="https://sc26.supercomputing.org/students/student-cluster-competition/">Student Cluster Competition</a> was held at the SC26 supercomputing conference in Chicago, where teams of six undergraduate students built and ran their own high-performance computing clusters. And the winner is TBD.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter wp-image-24284394 size-full" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.23.08-AM.png?resize=1224%2C729&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1224" height="729" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.23.08-AM.png?w=1224&amp;ssl=1 1224w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.23.08-AM.png?resize=300%2C179&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.23.08-AM.png?resize=1024%2C610&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.23.08-AM.png?resize=768%2C457&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-18-at-11.23.08-AM.png?resize=600%2C357&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Student Cluster Competition at SC26. Source: Brendan Heffernan/Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<p><strong>University at Albany</strong> and <strong>Swagelok Albany</strong> launched a <a href="https://www.albany.edu/news-center/news/2026-swagelok-albany-and-ualbanys-cnse-launch-workforce-training-partnership">semiconductor workforce training partnership</a> for lithography, advanced patterning, vacuum and plasma technologies, etch/deposition, metrology, and cleanroom operations.</p>
<p><strong>University of Arizona</strong>, Pima County, and Taiwan’s <strong>NYCU</strong> opened a new <a href="https://news.arizona.edu/news/arizona-taiwan-partnership-opens-tucson-semiconductor-hub">semiconductor talent and innovation hub</a> in Tucson for hands-on training, academic programs, exchanges, and networking with semiconductor employers.</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Determining not only whether a device meets specification, but how far it is from the boundary, helps inform product robustness and future design decisions.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/beyond-pass-fail-characterizing-silicon-margin-with-pvtw/">Beyond Pass/Fail: Characterizing Silicon Margin With PVTW</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Production test is necessarily built around decisions. PASS. FAIL. Bin 1. Bin 2. Ship. Do not ship. Characterization has a different objective. It asks not only whether the device meets specification, but how far it is from the boundary.</p>
<p>That distinction becomes increasingly important as processors integrate compute, memory, high speed interfaces, multiple power domains and advanced packaging into increasingly complex systems.</p>
<p>Suppose five devices all pass the product specification, but under the same controlled workload their minimum stable operating voltages are:</p>
<p>0.70 V | 0.71 V | 0.73 V | 0.76 V | 0.79 V</p>
<p>Production test sees five passing devices. Characterization sees five different levels of margin. That changes the engineering question from “Why did this device fail?” to something potentially much more useful:</p>
<p><strong>What is consuming product margin?</strong></p>
<p>PVTW (process, voltage, temperature, workload) gives us a framework for exploring that question.</p>
<p>Take the same device and establish its voltage and frequency boundary using conventional ATE content. Then establish another boundary while running a deterministic compute workload. Repeat with SRAM, memory, PCIe and combined workloads. We may discover that the boundaries are different.</p>
<p>That difference can tell us which workload is exposing the limiting condition and where deeper characterization should begin.</p>
<h2>Characterize the boundary</h2>
<p>Rather than testing at one nominal operating point, deliberately approach the boundary. Sweep voltage at fixed frequency, temperature and workload. Sweep frequency while the other conditions remain fixed. Repeat across temperature. Then manipulate individual power domains.</p>
<p>A compute workload may be characterized while the core rail is swept and memory and I/O rails remain fixed. A memory-intensive workload can be characterized while a memory-related rail is swept. PCIe traffic can be maintained while relevant electrical and thermal conditions are changed.</p>
<p>Now we are not simply determining whether a subsystem functions. We are measuring how much margin it has under a defined workload.</p>
<h2>Known good versus marginal populations</h2>
<p>Population analysis makes this more powerful. Take known good devices and devices showing reduced SLT margin. Characterize both populations under identical PVTW conditions. Instead of asking only which devices fail, compare the shapes of their operating envelopes.</p>
<p>Does the marginal population show a consistent voltage shift? Does the difference emerge only at high temperature? Does it appear only under simultaneous compute and memory activity? Is there a particular rail or frequency transition where the two populations separate?</p>
<p>The shape of the margin distribution can provide clues to the physical mechanism.</p>
<h2>A long SLT failure can become a short test</h2>
<p>Suppose a particular system workload requires 45 minutes before marginal devices begin generating errors. That is useful for learning, but expensive as a production screen.</p>
<p>PVTW characterization can help determine why that workload exposes the problem. Once the mechanism is better understood, the ATE capability can search for a shorter electrical, structural or functional signature correlated with the same marginality.</p>
<p>Perhaps a voltage and frequency corner exposes it in seconds. Perhaps a targeted functional pattern reproduces the condition. Perhaps a particular power rail response or interface measurement predicts it.</p>
<p>If the correlation is strong enough, the shorter test can potentially move into Final Test or even Wafer Sort. The learning flow becomes:</p>
<p>SLT discovers → PVTW characterizes → ATE correlates → Production test screens</p>
<p>The goal of deeper SLT characterization is therefore not necessarily more SLT. In some cases, its greatest value may be teaching us how to screen the risk earlier and faster.</p>
<h2>Margin is a continuous engineering variable</h2>
<p>This is why I believe we should preserve margin data rather than reducing everything immediately to PASS and FAIL. Minimum operating voltage, maximum stable frequency, thermal margin, interface margin, power margin and workload margin can all become continuous characterization parameters. Those measurements can be compared across device populations, process corners, assembly conditions and eventually manufacturing history.</p>
<p>A device with 100 mV of margin and a device with 10 mV of margin may both satisfy the production specification today. But they may tell us very different things about process variation, product robustness and future design decisions. Characterizing those differences can help answer a more fundamental question:</p>
<p><strong>Where is the product operating boundary, and what moves it?</strong></p>
<p>That question does not end at the silicon. The package surrounding the silicon has its own manufacturing history, materials and process variation.</p>
<p>In Part 3, I will explore how 2D ID and unit level traceability could connect PVTW margin all the way back to wafer X,Y and substrate panel X,Y.</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://semiengineering.com/pvtw-extending-silicon-characterization-from-pvt-to-real-world-workload/">Part 1: PVTW: Extending Silicon Characterization From PVT To Real-World Workload</a></li>
</ul>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/beyond-pass-fail-characterizing-silicon-margin-with-pvtw/">Beyond Pass/Fail: Characterizing Silicon Margin With PVTW</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>AI Accelerators Drive Changes In Power Delivery Networks</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/ai-accelerators-drive-changes-in-power-delivery-networks/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/ai-accelerators-drive-changes-in-power-delivery-networks/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Laura Peters]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 08:05:17 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Manufacturing, Packaging and Materials]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[800V DC]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI accelerators]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amkor]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[proteanTecs]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[voltage droop]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284319</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Moving voltage regulators closer to processors can reduce power loss, but adds challenges for advanced packaging, thermal management, and system-level design.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-accelerators-drive-changes-in-power-delivery-networks/">AI Accelerators Drive Changes In Power Delivery Networks</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>AI accelerators and GPUs consume thousands of amperes at increasingly low voltages, causing a shift to vertical power delivery that brings regulation closer to loads.</li>
<li>Advances in integrated voltage regulators and passive components are enabling new power architectures.</li>
<li>Power delivery is becoming a system-level co-design challenge, expanding simulation needs and the role of OSATs.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>Power delivery is now a significant performance limiter in data center chips. The demands of AI and high-performance computing chips running near 0.7 V for a 600W GPU are forcing current to rise to 1,000 amps and beyond, compounding resistive losses and voltage droop across the PDN. Multi-chiplet constructions only compound these issues by further increasing current density and signal congestion.</p>
<p>To solve these challenges, designers are turning to vertical power delivery networks, where the voltage regulator modules are relocated much closer to the processors from their position on the device periphery — to the PCB landside (under the PCB), inside the accelerator package, or inside the silicon interposer. Together with the growing customization of AI accelerator modules, there are many approaches on offer with varying design and manufacturing complexity.</p>
<p>The vertical PDN trend began nearly a decade ago with the integration of backside power delivery networks (BPDNs) on the wafer backside, enabling fine-tuned power delivery more directly to the transistors while slashing IR drop across the tens of interconnect levels on-chip. That approach is being extended with increasingly sophisticated on-die decoupling capacitors (DCaps) formed in deep trenches as part of the BPDN. While this addresses high-frequency decoupling at the chip level, recent changes inside packages, interposers, substrates, and across data center racks are becoming just as important.</p>
<p>Add to that recent developments in the design of compact passive devices, like high-performance inductors, and new architectural solutions become possible, moving the VRMs and DC-DC converters into the interposer or package.</p>
<p>But these architectural changes are neither straightforward nor simple because they involve system co-design of the PDN, interposer, power management ICs (PMICs), integrated voltage regulators (IVRs), DC-DC converters, capacitors, and other passive devices, alongside custom HPC, memory devices, and interconnects that perform today&#8217;s elaborate AI tasks.</p>
<p>“In the most advanced semiconductors, people are moving from discrete PMICs to distributed IVRs. Those IVRs are great because they are placed much closer to the chip, which reduces the IR drop to the chip’s own power delivery network, so it is way more efficient. In addition, closed and fast control loops between the chip power management unit to the IVR reduce the reaction time to voltage droop events from milliseconds to microseconds. Because this reaction is so fast you can tune voltage very accurately, with a high degree of granularity even on different areas of a chip,” said Nir Sever, senior director of business development at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/proteantecs/">proteanTecs</a>.</p>
<p>Reconfiguring the PDN is part of a much larger drive to improve energy use. “Energy efficiency becomes the most important performance index for AI-infused platforms moving forward,” said Lipen Yuan, senior director of TSMC’s Advanced Technology Business Development, during a 2024 IEDM short course.</p>
<p>Power delivery challenges are most important at points of load. “AI systems are placing unprecedented demands on board-to-chip power delivery, making the final stage of voltage regulation increasingly important,” said CP Hung, vice president of corporate R&amp;D at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/ase/">ASE</a>.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284321" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn1.png?resize=936%2C510&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="936" height="510" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn1.png?w=936&amp;ssl=1 936w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn1.png?resize=300%2C163&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn1.png?resize=768%2C418&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn1.png?resize=600%2C327&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 936px) 100vw, 936px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 1: By regulating voltage at the point of load, OSATs enable fine-tuned voltage control. Source: ASE</strong></p>
<p>“The move to multi-die architectures is bringing power integrity to the forefront, alongside thermal, stress, and warpage challenges. But the underlying power-delivery problem already existed in advanced single-die designs,” said Sojan Philips, principal product manager of Redhawk-SC at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. “AI accelerators are now operating at currents approaching the kiloampere range, where even milliohm-level resistance in the power-delivery network can result in significant IR drop. That loss of voltage margin can directly affect performance and power targets, particularly maximum operating frequency and V<sub>min</sub>.”</p>
<p>Several developments illustrate these trends:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Backside PDN</strong>: Intel Foundry&#8217;s 18A was the first to move backside wafer processing into leading-edge high-volume manufacturing. Samsung and TSMC plan to implement backside PDNs at future advanced nodes, starting next year.</li>
<li><strong>Integrated voltage regulators</strong>: ASE, Marvell, Empower, and others are moving voltage regulation closer to the processor.</li>
<li><strong>Embedded capacitors and inductors</strong>: Integration of compact passive components is becoming a key differentiator in high-density power delivery.</li>
<li><strong>Co-optimization of package and on-chip power</strong>: Power integrity can no longer be optimized independently at the PCB, substrate, interposer, and transistor levels.</li>
</ul>
<p>As a result, OSATs are playing an increasing role in system co-design, electrical validation, and power integrity (PI) simulation. “The OSAT sits where die, substrate or interposer, passives, and board meet, so we are brought in earlier to model the package-level PDN and co-simulate it with the customer&#8217;s die power data,” said Alex Kizeev, technical director of signal and power integrity at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/amkor-technology/">Amkor’s</a> design optimization team.</p>
<p>Kizeev breaks down the supply chain and responsibilities among different companies. “The best results come from a PDN budget partitioned across the die, package, and board. The foundry and fabless teams own the on-die PDN, including the die power map and the bump-map allocation. The substrate supplier defines what stackup and via/plane technology is possible. The OSAT optimizes the package-level pieces, including stackup choice, via and ball assignment, DCap selection and placement, warpage and reliability, and validation.”</p>
<p>&#8220;Backside power delivery can significantly improve power delivery by reducing IR drop and freeing up frontside routing resources for signals, but it doesn’t eliminate power-integrity challenges,” said Philips. “It also changes the thermal landscape. As power delivery and other structures move to the backside, the paths available for heat removal become more complex. This makes co-optimization of power integrity and thermal management increasingly important.</p>
<p>The overall trend is moving toward closer integration in advanced packages and dies, including the integration of inductors, capacitors, and power-conversion components into substrates and interposers.</p>
<p><strong>Why power losses scale with increasing power</strong><br />
A simple illustration shows how the power efficiency drops as the industry scales from a 1kW GPU to a 5kW GPU (see figure 2). While losses from the voltage regulator itself stay constant at around 13%, resistive losses jump from 8% to 31%, and voltage droop rises from 17% to 31%. As a result, increasing power levels must be supplied to meet all the chip-level needs.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284322" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn2.png?resize=936%2C378&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="936" height="378" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn2.png?w=936&amp;ssl=1 936w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn2.png?resize=300%2C121&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn2.png?resize=768%2C310&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn2.png?resize=600%2C242&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 936px) 100vw, 936px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 2: At higher power levels, the PDN efficiency using motherboard voltage regulators degrades significantly due to VR losses, resistive losses, and droop. Source: Intel Foundry</strong></p>
<p>With this lateral power delivery approach, power integrity (PI) becomes increasingly difficult to maintain as compute loads rise. As AI accelerators integrate increasing levels of compute, HBM, and interconnects between devices, the low supply voltage (0.7-0.9V) of devices means multiple kiloamp levels of current are needed to meet the high-power needs of modern GPUs. As if managing kiloamp currents is not enough, fast workload transients tend to magnify voltage droop, so designers apply voltage guardbands that further increase power consumption and reduce system efficiency. [1]</p>
<p><strong>Next-gen data centers pull in 800V</strong><br />
For next-generation data centers built to meet AI demands, 800V DC power delivery is starting to replace traditional AC power distribution. The 800V DC power distribution coexists with multiple distributed regulators located much closer to individual loads.</p>
<p>To lower transmission losses from the grid to the microprocessor, next-generation PDNs begin at 800V DC and are stepped down in stages using DC-to-DC converters to 48V, 12V, and then 0.7V, for instance. One of the most critical junctures occurs at the point of load, where integrated voltage regulators supply the AI multi-chiplet package. By limiting the number of power conversions, system-level efficiency can improve while reducing thermal cooling requirements.</p>
<p>The voltage regulator module can be positioned underneath the PCB, a logical first step that is commonly referred to as vertical power delivery. However, chipmakers are embracing recent developments that shrink the size of various voltage regulator components, especially capacitors and inductors, to move voltage regulation inside the main processor package and below the SoC, called integrated power delivery. Finally, integrating voltage regulator components into the interposer itself is called fully integrated power delivery.</p>
<p><strong>One step at a time</strong><br />
The approach companies take to voltage regulation integration depends on the company&#8217;s expertise, its partners, and the complexity of the system involved.</p>
<p>For this reason, companies are introducing suites of solutions. For example, ASE is offering a range of power delivery capabilities to enable PCB backside integration, which considers the limited space inside computer trays. [2] The company’s PowerSiP options can include double-sided connected capacitors sandwiched between coreless substrates; substrateless designs, solder-free cap array modules with exposed terminals for embedded substrate or PCB solutions; high-aspect-ratio 4:1 copper pin integration for lower packaging impedance; and high-density active and passive component integration using single- or double-sided molding.</p>
<p>“Power efficiency is becoming a defining challenge for next-generation AI systems,” said Harrison Chang, vice president of corporate R&amp;D at ASE. “By shortening the high-current transmission path, our solution can reduce parasitic routing losses by 50% or more, translating directly into meaningful power savings at the system level,” he said in the release.</p>
<p>In June 2025, Marvell introduced its Package Integrated Voltage Regulator (PIVR) technology for AI and HPC. The PIVR replaces the conventional arrangement of discrete voltage-regulator components, often several inches away on the PCB, with compact silicon power converters and passive components tightly coupled to the processor. It’s designed to reduce the amount of low-voltage, high-current routing between the regulator and the processor. The PIVR can sit on the underside of the processor package, shortening the path from voltage conversion to the processor&#8217;s power connections. It also can sit next to the processor’s package or inside the advanced package.</p>
<p>Marvell is partnering with multiple companies on integration, including Infineon, which makes power semiconductors and packaged devices; Ferric, which specializes in designing integrated voltage regulators using thin-film magnetic inductors; Monolithic Power Systems, a developer of high-current-density power modules; and Empower Semiconductor, a developer of compact integrated power converters.</p>
<p>In July of this year, Analog Devices completed the acquisition of Empower Semiconductor, expanding ADI’s AI compute power delivery capabilities with integrated voltage regulators and silicon capacitors. “By enabling power conversion closer to the processor, the combined solution shortens the power delivery path and improves efficiency to support higher-performance, higher-density systems. Building on its leadership in high-performance power management, ADI is investing in its system-level platform to deliver a step-change in performance, density, and efficiency from grid to core.” [3]</p>
<p>In addition, on Nov. 2, onsemi announced it will acquire the assets of Ferric, which develops integrated voltage regulators, for up to $775 million. [4]</p>
<p>To understand these important acquisitions, it helps to take a closer look at IVRs.</p>
<p><strong>What is an IVR?</strong><br />
An integrated voltage regulator is typically a 3D stack arrangement that replaces off-chip voltage regulator modules and bulky capacitors and inductors on the PCB with integrated magnetics and passives mounted onto the silicon or package using thin-film technology.</p>
<p>“The IVR is implemented as a 3D stack, bringing together the deep-trench capacitor, PMIC, and inductor, and embedding them directly into the substrate or interposer,” said Veshal Sridhar, product engineering architect, 3D IC at Synopsys. “Companies are moving it closer to the SOC for obvious reasons such as reduced signal parasitics between IVR and SOC die, but that introduces new thermal effects and stress effects because you&#8217;re bringing another 3D stack in and embedding it inside an interposer. There’s also potential noise coupling into the other dies from the IVR, because even for a simple 3D stack you are going to have noise coupling.”</p>
<p>In what Intel Foundry refers to as a Super High Density MIM (metal-insulator-metal), capacitance density can be improved by 10× compared to conventional MIM capacitors. [5] This may offer response times on the order of nanoseconds. As part of Intel’s collaboration with UMC for 12nm finFETs, the capacitors feature three metal layers for routing in a scalable design. “This broad adaptability highlights the HDMIM as a universal, highly scalable passive component, capable of heterogeneous integration with logic and power chips manufactured across diverse process nodes from various foundries.” [5]</p>
<p>Inductors have traditionally been one of the greatest passive bottlenecks due to their large footprint, but by integrating them into the package using copper-plated through-hole technology and advanced magnetic materials (see Figure 3), Intel is touting a high resistance-to-capacitance ratio while minimizing stray magnetic fields that could adversely affect surrounding circuits. [5]</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284323" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn3.png?resize=830%2C354&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="830" height="354" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn3.png?w=830&amp;ssl=1 830w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn3.png?resize=300%2C128&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn3.png?resize=768%2C328&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/pdn3.png?resize=600%2C256&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 830px) 100vw, 830px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 3: This in-package inductor uses copper-plated through holes embedded with high-permeability magnetics to improve the resistance-to-capacitance ratio and limit magnetic parasitic effects. Source: Intel Foundry</strong></p>
<p>Intel’s FIVR platform uses switched-capacitor voltage regulators and hybrid buck converters to accommodate nanosecond transient spikes by computing cores without exacerbating voltage droop. FIVRs that allow a continuous switching range are superior to fixed SCVRs when it comes to delivering low-voltage inputs (e.g., 1.8 to 3V).</p>
<p>Finally, Intel’s low-voltage GaN MOSHEMT-on-CMOS devices are designed for &gt;12V applications, where high density and high efficiency are essential, while minimizing the overall number of power conversions. By integrating both low voltage and high voltage MIM capacitors with active devices, it minimizes PDN impedance from VR to load. By monolithically integrating the GaN power switches with GaN nMOS drivers, the company can achieve even faster response to transients along with aggressive power density.</p>
<p>“Integrated voltage regulators provide unprecedented speed and granularity in voltage control, but realizing their full power-saving potential requires equally precise visibility into the silicon itself,” said proteanTecs&#8217; Sever. “Traditional power management methods leave significant timing margin untapped. By continuously measuring timing margins across the full chip, cycle by cycle, under actual operating conditions, our embedded monitoring technology identifies where performance is truly limited and where voltage can be safely reduced. Combined with the fine-grained control of IVRs, this enables more aggressive power optimization without compromising performance or reliability.”</p>
<p><strong>Can the system handle the power?</strong><br />
Multiphysics simulation plays an important role in power delivery planning. “A designer may have a simple stack in mind, interposer, base die, top die, and substrate. And it starts with a prototype grid and listing the power required for each die,” explained Synopsys’ Sridhar. “So there is a power budget that comes from the devices, and number of C4 bumps, number of microbumps, TSVs, etc. You want to determine whether your power delivery system can deliver the power needed from the C4 bumps through the top of the stack. Starting with a simple static IR-drop analysis, we can then leverage 3DIC Compiler together with signoff solutions like RedHawk-SC to help bridge the gap between early bump planning and PSE (power sourcing equipment) planning.”</p>
<p>In cases where the PDN cannot handle the power required of the system, design changes such as increasing the interposer size may come into play.</p>
<p>Sridhar noted how one customer brought system-level PI (power integrity) analysis into the early architecture stage, closing the loop between implementation and system analysis using 3DICC plus RHSC, turning iterations that once took days into hours and giving designers more freedom to explore before the architecture is frozen.</p>
<p>At the same time, the package assembly design kits that OSATs compile are becoming more detailed. “Kits are moving beyond geometric design rules to include electrical models, material properties, and validated reference designs. Teams now must consider bump pitch against current density, metal layers against cost, and DCap placement against thermal path early in the design,” said Amkor’s Kizeev.</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion </strong><br />
Power integrity and power efficiency have already become key differentiators in advanced AI accelerator-based data center designs. Companies are racing to incorporate leading power delivery solutions, including integrated voltage regulators, in positions closer to the GPU or AI accelerator modules where they can be most effective. Advanced power delivery began with the integration of wafer backside PDNs, but package-level and interposer-level solutions are coming fast.</p>
<p>Coordination among major companies in the supply chain is critical during early-stage PDN design. “Some of these design houses are not building the entire system, for instance, like HBM, where even the base die and the stack may be coming in from other design houses,” noted Synopsys’ Phillips. They want to make sure that the grid they&#8217;re designing in-house works with the other models. So they want a model exchange between these two, which is performed as part of a feasibility analysis.”</p>
<p><strong>References</strong></p>

Moving the power delivery network to the backside of a chip reduces congestion, but it introduces new challenges for fabs.</p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/gan-power-devices-go-vertical/">GaN Power Devices Go Vertical</a><br />
Why new designs and process flows could help overcome manufacturing challenges.</p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/new-approaches-to-power-decoupling/">New Approaches To Power Decoupling</a><br />
Strategic placement of decoupling capacitors and regulators is critical to keeping power stable, but no one approach is sufficient.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ai-accelerators-drive-changes-in-power-delivery-networks/">AI Accelerators Drive Changes In Power Delivery Networks</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<post-id xmlns="com-wordpress:feed-additions:1">24284319</post-id>	</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Virtual Twins And Machine Learning Optimize DRAM Yield</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/how-virtual-twins-and-machine-learning-optimize-dram-yield/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/how-virtual-twins-and-machine-learning-optimize-dram-yield/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Taeyeon Oh]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 08:03:18 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - MD]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Manufacturing, Packaging and Materials]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Perfecting The Process]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[digital twins]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[DRAM]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Lam Research]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[machine learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[metrology]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ML]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[process modeling]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[virtual fabrication]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[virtual metrology]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[virtual twins]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[yield]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24284379</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Model process variation, detect multiple failure modes, and evaluate solutions with less wafer experimentation.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/how-virtual-twins-and-machine-learning-optimize-dram-yield/">How Virtual Twins And Machine Learning Optimize DRAM Yield</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>As memory technologies continue to scale, manufacturing processes are becoming increasingly complex.</p>
<p>Additional process steps, tighter process windows, and new sources of variation are making it progressively more challenging to reach acceptable yield.</p>
<p>Systemic failures are emerging as a source of yield loss during yield ramp. Unlike random defects, these failures are often driven by complex interactions between multiple process modules.</p>
<p>Rapid root-cause identification of these systemic defects is critical to quickly improving yield and accelerating time-to-market.<sup>1</sup> But conventional failure analysis relies heavily on wafer experimentation and statistical analysis, which can be slow and costly as manufacturing complexity increases.</p>
<p>That’s where calibrated virtual twins and machine-learning optimization can help.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284380" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig1.webp?resize=914%2C722&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="914" height="722" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig1.webp?w=914&amp;ssl=1 914w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig1.webp?resize=300%2C237&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig1.webp?resize=768%2C607&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig1.webp?resize=600%2C474&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 914px) 100vw, 914px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Systemic failures are a key bottleneck during yield ramp. Faster identification and reduction of these failures can accelerate the yield ramp and shorten the time required to reach a targeted manufacturing yield.</strong></p>
<p>Process engineers use virtual manufacturing models (<a href="https://www.lamresearch.com/product/semulator3d/">SEMulator3D</a>) with machine learning (ML) optimization (<a href="https://newsroom.lamresearch.com/fabtex-yield-optimizer-improves-processes-for-high-volume-manufacturing?blog=true">Fabtex Yield Optimizer</a>) to predict process failures.</p>
<ul>
<li>A virtual twin can improve DRAM yield analysis by reproducing process variation in a calibrated digital environment.</li>
<li>ML can then evaluate multiple failure mechanisms and process changes simultaneously.</li>
</ul>
<p>Using this technique, engineers can evaluate potential yield improvements, all with limited need for wafer-based experimentation.</p>
<h2>Detecting multiple DRAM failure modes with virtual metrology</h2>
<p>As an example of this technique, we built a SEMulator3D model (virtual twin) of a DRAM manufacturing process sequence.  During this model-building, we established virtual metrology measurements throughout the process sequence (Figure 2).</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284381" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig2.webp?resize=1309%2C747&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1309" height="747" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig2.webp?w=1309&amp;ssl=1 1309w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig2.webp?resize=300%2C171&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig2.webp?resize=1024%2C584&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig2.webp?resize=768%2C438&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig2.webp?resize=600%2C342&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Building and calibrating a DRAM virtual twin using process integration, virtual metrology, and silicon data.</strong></p>
<p>ML was then used to calibrate the model against silicon data, enabling the virtual environment to better reproduce observed manufacturing behavior and serve as the foundation for downstream failure analysis and optimization.</p>
<h2>Building and calibrating a DRAM virtual twin</h2>
<p>For our specific case study, we built a calibrated virtual twin for our DRAM memory device that contained eight known systemic failure mechanisms.</p>
<p>Figure 3 displays examples of these failure mechanisms, including shorts, opens, and overlap-related defects between critical DRAM structures.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284382" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig3.webp?resize=1281%2C692&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1281" height="692" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig3.webp?w=1281&amp;ssl=1 1281w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig3.webp?resize=300%2C162&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig3.webp?resize=1024%2C553&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig3.webp?resize=768%2C415&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig3.webp?resize=600%2C324&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 3: Reproducing systemic DRAM failure modes and detecting them using virtual metrology. </strong></p>
<p>We also specified (in the virtual twin) the virtual metrology needed to detect these failures. By translating each failure into a measurable geometric condition and monitoring it through virtual metrology, we were able to automatically detect and simultaneously analyze multiple failure mechanisms within a single study.</p>
<h2>Using machine learning to optimize process targets</h2>
<p>Using the calibrated virtual twin, ML was then applied to help us understand the impact of process and structural variations on the eight predefined failure modes.</p>
<p>We then searched for an optimized manufacturing solution (specific process changes) that provided the lowest failure rate across our eight predefined failure modes. Instead of addressing each failure mode independently, the optimization routine evaluated the impact of multiple process changes simultaneously and searched for process targets that improved the overall pass ratio.</p>
<h2>Case study results: Predicted yield increased to 78.5%</h2>
<p>As shown in Figure 4, the optimized process settings significantly reduced all eight systemic failure modes.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24284383" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig4.webp?resize=1332%2C489&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1332" height="489" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig4.webp?w=1332&amp;ssl=1 1332w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig4.webp?resize=300%2C110&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig4.webp?resize=1024%2C376&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig4.webp?resize=768%2C282&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Lam_Virtual-Twins-ML-DRAM-Yield-fig4.webp?resize=600%2C220&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 4: Simultaneous reduction of multiple systemic failures through ML-based optimization and retargeting analysis. A sample retargeting plot for SNLP (storage node landing pad) open/short failures is highlighted.</strong></p>
<p>Under the nominal process conditions used in the study, the predicted total yield was 0.1%. Following optimization, the predicted yield increased to 78.5%, with lower failure rates across all eight failure mechanisms.</p>
<p>This analysis also translated the optimized solution into actionable inline fab targets that can be directly applied in the real process, helping engineers identify which process and structural parameters should be adjusted to further reduce systemic failures.</p>
<h2>Accelerating semiconductor yield improvement</h2>
<p>This work demonstrates how the combination of process models and ML can be used to accelerate yield improvement during DRAM device development. By reproducing process variability in a calibrated virtual environment, and performing simultaneous optimization and retargeting, engineers can detect multiple device failures and investigate a broad solution space without the time and cost of extensive silicon experimentation.</p>
<h3>Reference</h3>
<p><sup>1</sup> I. Tirkel, &#8220;Yield Learning Curve Models in Semiconductor Manufacturing,&#8221; IEEE Transactions on Semiconductor Manufacturing, vol. 26, no. 4, pp. 564-571, Nov. 2013.</p>
